=== rifAMPin (Merck Manual Professional) ===
Source: https://www.merckmanuals.com/professional/drug-names-generic-and-brand (productid 545)
Retrieved: 2026-09-25
NURSING QUICK-REFERENCE SUMMARY
Auto-generated on 2026-09-25 from the prescribing information in this file; full details follow below.

Drug Class: Rifampin (rifampicin), a semisynthetic rifamycin antibiotic, is primarily used as part of first-line, combination therapy in the treatment of tuberculosis.

Routes: oral, injection (IV/IM/SC); Common adult doses: 10 mg/kg/day (Max: 600 mg/day) PO/IV for tuberculosis treatment; 1,200 mg/day PO/IV for meningococcal prophylaxis.

Common Side Effects (excluding routine GI upset): pyrosis (heartburn), hematuria, hemolysis, hyperuricemia, interstitial nephritis, renal failure (unspecified), renal tubular necrosis, contact lens discoloration, tooth discoloration, urine discoloration, cholestasis, elevated hepatic enzymes, hepatic failure, hepatitis, hepatotoxicity, hyperbilirubinemia, jaundice, ataxia, confusion, dizziness, drowsiness, fatigue, headache, myopathy, psychosis, visual impairment, weakness, agranulocytosis, anemia, bleeding

In an Overdose: No overdose/antidote information is listed in this file.

Key Nursing Considerations: Contraindications/precautions: Rifampin should not be administered to patients with known rifamycin hypersensitivity, including rifabutin, because cross-sensitivity between agents is possible.This drug does not treat viral infections (e.g., common cold). Prescribing rifampin in the absence of a proven or strongly suspected...

Brand Names
  - Rifadin
  - Rifadin IV
  - Rimactane

Dosage Forms
  - Rifadin 150mg Capsule
  - Rifadin IV 600mg Powder for Injection
  - Rifadin IV 600mg Powder for Injection (NOVAPLUS)
  - Rifampin 150mg Oral capsule
  - Rifampin 300mg Oral capsule
  - Rifampin 600mg Lyophilisate for solution for injection
  - Rifampin Bulk powder

Indication Specific Dosing
  Indications
    Related Indications
      Heading
      for the treatment of drug-susceptible tuberculosis infection in persons without HIV as part of combination therapy
      Routes
        Heading
        Oral dosage
        Route Age Groups
          Heading
          Adults
          Dosage Description
          10 mg/kg/dose (Max: 600 mg/dose) PO once daily or 5 days/week, or alternatively, 3 days/week or twice weekly. Daily dosing is preferred and is defined as 5- or 7 days/week. Rifampin is generally recommended throughout the intensive and continuation phases of treatment as first-line therapy; duration is dependent on the site of involvement.
          Heading
          Adolescents
          Dosage Description
          10 to 20 mg/kg/dose (Max: 600 mg/dose) PO once daily or 5 days/week, or alternatively, 3 days/week or twice weekly. 20 to 30 mg/kg/dose (Max: 600 mg/dose) PO once daily may be considered for disseminated disease. Daily dosing is preferred and is defined as 5 or 7 days/week. Rifampin is generally recommended throughout the intensive and continuation phases of treatment as first-line therapy; duration is dependent on the site of involvement.
          Heading
          Children 3 to 12 years
          Dosage Description
          10 to 20 mg/kg/dose (Max: 600 mg/dose) PO once daily or 5 days/week, or alternatively, 3 days/week or twice weekly. 20 to 30 mg/kg/dose (Max: 600 mg/dose) PO once daily may be considered for disseminated disease. Daily dosing is preferred and is defined as 5 or 7 days/week. Rifampin is generally recommended throughout the intensive and continuation phases of treatment as first-line therapy; duration is dependent on the site of involvement.
          Heading

          Dosage Description
          20 to 30 mg/kg/dose PO once daily or 5 days/week, or alternatively, 3 days/week or twice weekly. Daily dosing is preferred and is defined as 5 or 7 days/week. Rifampin is generally recommended throughout the intensive and continuation phases of treatment as first-line therapy; duration is dependent on the site of involvement.
          Heading
          Neonates†
          Dosage Description
          10 to 20 mg/kg/dose PO once daily. Although tuberculosis is rare in neonates, congenital and postnatal cases have been successfully treated with antitubercular agents. Rifampin is generally recommended throughout the intensive and continuation phases of treatment as first-line therapy; duration is dependent on the site of involvement.

    Routes
    Heading
    For tuberculosis prophylaxis† or latent tuberculosis infection† (LTBI)
    Routes
      Heading
      Oral dosage
      Route Age Groups
        Heading
        Adults
        Dosage Description
        600 mg PO every 12 hours for 2 days. Initiate prophylaxis as soon as possible after exposure (ideally less than 24 hours after identification of index patient); prophylaxis initiated more than 14 days after onset of illness in the index patient has very limited or no value.
        Heading
        Infants, Children, and Adolescents
        Dosage Description
        10 mg/kg/dose (Max: 600 mg/dose) PO every 12 hours for 2 days. Initiate prophylaxis as soon as possible after exposure (ideally less than 24 hours after identification of index patient); prophylaxis initiated more than 14 days after onset of illness in the index patient has very limited or no value.
        Heading
        Neonates
        Dosage Description
        5 mg/kg/dose PO every 12 hours for 2 days. Initiate prophylaxis as soon as possible after exposure (ideally less than 24 hours after identification of index patient); prophylaxis initiated more than 14 days after onset of illness in the index patient has very limited or no value.
    Heading
    For post-exposure meningococcal infection prophylaxis†

      Heading
      for the monthly treatment of paucibacillary leprosy (Hansen's disease)†
      Routes
        Heading
        Oral dosage
        Route Age Groups
          Heading
          Adults
          Dosage Description
          600 mg PO once monthly in combination with dapsone for 12 months. Alternatively, 600 mg PO once monthly in combination with dapsone and clofazimine for 6 months. Monthly treatment is recommended for those receiving prednisone and those receiving treatment through the WHO's Global Leprosy Program. The WHO's Global Leprosy Program also allows for shorter treatment durations. Treatment that is more intensive and of longer duration is medically preferable.
          Heading
          Adolescents 15 to 17 years
          Dosage Description

Monthly treatment is recommended for those receiving prednisone and those receiving treatment through the WHO's Global Leprosy Program. The WHO's Global Leprosy Program also allows for shorter treatment durations. Treatment that is more intensive and of longer duration is medically preferable.

          Heading
          Infants, Children, and Adolescents 3 months to 17 years
          Dosage Description

Contraindications And Precautions
  Headline
  Contraindications/Warnings/Precautions
  General Information
  Rifampin should not be administered to patients with known rifamycin hypersensitivity, including rifabutin, because cross-sensitivity between agents is possible.This drug does not treat viral infections (e.g., common cold). Prescribing rifampin in the absence of a proven or strongly suspected bacterial infection or a prophylactic indication is unlikely to provide benefit to the patient and increases the risk of the development of drug-resistant bacteria. Patients should be told to complete the full course of treatment, even if they feel better earlier.Although Clostridium difficile has been shown in vitro to be sensitive to rifampin, pseudomembranous colitis has been reported with the use of rifampin as well as almost all antibacterial agents have been associated with pseudomembranous colitis (antibiotic-associated colitis) which may range in severity from mild to life-threatening. In the colon, overgrowth of Clostridia may exist when normal flora is altered subsequent to antibacterial administration. The toxin produced by Clostridium difficile is a primary cause of pseudomembranous colitis. It is known that systemic use of antibiotics predisposes patients to development of pseudomembranous colitis. Consideration should be given to the diagnosis of pseudomembranous colitis in patients presenting with diarrhea following antibacterial administration. Systemic antibiotics should be prescribed with caution to patients with inflammatory bowel disease such as ulcerative colitis or other GI disease. If diarrhea develops during therapy, the drug should be discontinued. Following diagnosis of pseudomembranous colitis, therapeutic measures should be instituted. In milder cases, the colitis may respond to discontinuation of the offending agent. In moderate to severe cases, fluids and electrolytes, protein supplementation, and treatment with an antibacterial effective against Clostridium difficile may be warranted. Products inhibiting peristalsis are contraindicated in this clinical situation. Practitioners should be aware that antibiotic-associated colitis has been observed to occur over two months or more following discontinuation of systemic antibiotic therapy; a careful medical history should be taken.Transient increases in hepatic enzymes and severe, sometimes fatal, liver toxicity may occur during rifampin treatment. Rifampin should be used with caution in patients with chronic hepatic disease or alcoholism. Liver-function tests should be conducted prior to and every 2—4 weeks during treatment. Rifampin should be discontinued if signs or symptoms of hepatic damage become evident. In August 2003 the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) and the American Thoracic Society (ATS) treatment recommendations for latent tuberculosis infection (LTBI) were changed to indicate that the 2-month regimen of rifampin plus pyrazinamide (PZA) should generally not be offered, especially to patients with underlying hepatic disease (e.g., hepatitis, jaundice) or those with a history of isoniazid-associated liver injury. This recommendation is based on a total of 48 cases (between October 2000 and June 2003) of confirmed pyrazinamide-associated severe liver injury reported to CDC, with 11 cases being fatal. A subsequent survey found high rates of hospitalization and death from liver injury associated with the use of PZA. After a review of surveillance data, survey results, and recent published studies of PZA-associated liver injury, the ATS and CDC (with Infectious Diseases Society of America endorsement) revised their original recommendation to advise that the rifampin-PZA regimen should generally not be offered. Other regimens for the treatment of LTBI, which are known to be safe and effective, such as rifampin alone or isoniazid (INH), should be used. It is important to note that the recommendation against the use of PZA for treatment of LTBI does not apply to the appropriate use of rifampin and PZA in multidrug regimens for the treatment of persons with active TB disease. If PZA is used in combination with rifampin, liver function tests (LFTs) and bilirubin should be monitored (see PZA monograph). The CDC continues to collect reports of severe liver injury leading (those requiring hospitalization or leading to death) in persons receiving any treatment for LTBI. Health-care providers are encouraged to report such events to CDC's Division of Tuberculosis Elimination, telephone (404)—639—8442.Rifampin sterile powder for injection contains the sulfite sodium formaldehyde sulfoxylate. Although the incidence is low, serious or potentially fatal anaphylactoid reactions can be precipitated in individuals with known sulfite hypersensitivity or others, such as those with atopy, who receive IV rifampin. It does not appear that oral formulations of rifampin pose a similar risk.Rifampin can discolor bodily fluids such as urine, saliva, tears, and sputum. Patients should be warned of this occurrence. Wearers of soft contact lenses should be warned that the lenses may be permanently stained.Rifampin is classified as FDA pregnancy risk category C. Most studies involving pregnant women have substantiated the conclusion that rifampin is not a teratogen. Rifampin has been associated with hemorrhagic disease of the newborn. Rifampin is used in the treatment of tuberculosis in pregnant women; however, potential risks to the fetus should be considered. Although rifampin is probably safe, no efficacy data support its use for the prophylaxis of tuberculosis in pregnant women.Rifampin is distributed into breast milk, but adverse effects to the nursing infant have not been reported. The American Academy of Pediatrics considers rifampin to be compatible with breast-feeding.Rifampin has enzyme-inducing properties, including induction of delta amino levulinic acid synthetase. Isolated reports have associated rifampin administration with porphyria exacerbation.Due to the adverse drug-drug interactions that complicate the concurrent use of rifampin and protease inhibitors in patients with tuberculosis (TB) and concurrent human immunodeficiency virus (HIV) infection, the CDC recommends the use of rifabutin in place of rifampin in multidrug regimens for the treatment of active TB in patients with acquired immunodeficiency syndrome (AIDS). Until additional data become available, the CDC also recommends that persons with TB and advanced HIV infection with CD4+ counts < 100/mm3 not be treated with highly intermittent (i.e., once or twice-weekly) rifamycin regimens due to the occurrence of acquired rifamycin resistance. Acquired rifamycin resistance has been reported in a study of HIV-TB patients treated with twice-weekly rifampin and isoniazid. The consistency of the finding of rifamycin resistance among TB patients with advanced HIV disease suggests that once- or twice-weekly therapy including isoniazid and a rifamycin increases the risk for acquired rifamycin resistance.
  Contraindications And Precautions
    Headline
    Drug Interactions
    General Information
    The coadministration of certain medications may lead to harm and require avoidance or therapy modification; review all drug interactions prior to concomitant use of other medications.
    Headline
    Hypersensitivity
    General Information
    This medication is contraindicated in patients with a history of hypersensitivity to it or any of its components. Rifampin is also contraindicated in individuals with a history of hypersensitivity to any other rifamycin class antimicrobial agent.
    Headline
    contact lenses, dentures
    General Information
    Rifampin may produce a re-orange discoloration of body tissues and/or fluids (e.g., skin, teeth, tongue, urine, feces, saliva, sputum, tears, sweat, and cerebrospinal fluid). Contact lenses or dentures may become permanently stained due to rifampin therapy.
    Headline
    hepatic disease, malnutrition, vitamin K deficiency
    General Information
    Use rifampin only in cases of necessity and then under strict medical supervision in individuals with impaired liver function. Carefully monitor liver function before rifampin therapy and every 2 to 4 weeks during therapy in these individuals. Severe hepatic dysfunction, including fatalities, has been reported with rifampin in individuals with hepatic disease and in individuals receiving rifampin with other hepatotoxic agents. Additionally, rifampin may cause vitamin K-dependent coagulation disorders and bleeding. Monitor coagulation tests during rifampin treatment (e.g., prothrombin time and other coagulation tests) in individuals at risk of vitamin K deficiency, such as individuals with chronic hepatic disease or malnutrition or those on prolonged antibacterial drugs or anticoagulants. Most combination therapy for active TB disease includes more than 1 agent that may contribute to hepatotoxicity.
    Headline
    porphyria
    General Information
    Avoid rifampin in individuals with porphyria. Porphyria exacerbation has been reported with rifampin, attributed to induction of delta amino levulinic acid synthetase.
    Headline
    diabetes mellitus
    General Information
    Use rifampin with caution in individuals with a history of diabetes mellitus, as diabetes management may be more difficult.
    Headline
    pregnancy
    General Information
    Published data over decades of use have not established an association with the use of rifampin during pregnancy and major birth defects or miscarriage. However, there are no adequate and well-controlled trials of rifampin in pregnant individuals. Rifampin crosses the placental barrier and is present in cord blood. In animal studies, congenital malformations including spina bifida and cleft palate were increased in the offspring of pregnant rats who were given about 1 to 2 times and 0.2 to 0.8 times, respectively, the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD) based on body surface area. Imperfect osteogenesis and embryotoxicity were also observed in pregnant rabbits given oral doses up to about 3 times the MRHD. Tuberculosis guidelines support treatment initiation during pregnancy whenever the probability of disease is moderate to high due to the risk of untreated tuberculosis to both the pregnant individual and the fetus. Guidelines also state rifampin can be used for post-exposure prophylaxis of tularemia in pregnant individuals if first and second-line agents are not available. When given during the last few weeks of pregnancy, rifampin can cause postnatal hemorrhages in the pregnant individual and infant. Monitor clotting parameters in patients who are on rifamycins and consider prophylactic vitamin K if the patient is being treated for tuberculosis.
    Headline
    neonates and infants exposed to this medication in utero
    General Information
    Neonates and infants exposed to this medication in utero during the last few weeks of pregnancy may experience postnatal hemorrhage. Monitor the infant for bleeding and treat with vitamin K if indicated.
    Headline
    breast-feeding
    General Information
    Rifampin is compatible with breast-feeding. Guidelines encourage breast-feeding in individuals who are noninfectious and taking first-line tuberculosis agents, such as rifampin, as the small concentrations of these drugs measured in human milk have not been reported to produce adverse effects in the breastfed child. FDA-approved labeling recommends avoiding rifampin use during breast-feeding due to the potential to cause serious side effects such as tumorigenicity (based on animal studies) in the breast-fed child.

Pregnancy And Lactation
  Pregnancy
    General Information
    Published data over decades of use have not established an association with the use of rifampin during pregnancy and major birth defects or miscarriage. However, there are no adequate and well-controlled trials of rifampin in pregnant individuals. Rifampin crosses the placental barrier and is present in cord blood. In animal studies, congenital malformations including spina bifida and cleft palate were increased in the offspring of pregnant rats who were given about 1 to 2 times and 0.2 to 0.8 times, respectively, the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD) based on body surface area. Imperfect osteogenesis and embryotoxicity were also observed in pregnant rabbits given oral doses up to about 3 times the MRHD. Tuberculosis guidelines support treatment initiation during pregnancy whenever the probability of disease is moderate to high due to the risk of untreated tuberculosis to both the pregnant individual and the fetus. Guidelines also state rifampin can be used for post-exposure prophylaxis of tularemia in pregnant individuals if first and second-line agents are not available. When given during the last few weeks of pregnancy, rifampin can cause postnatal hemorrhages in the pregnant individual and infant. Monitor clotting parameters in patients who are on rifamycins and consider prophylactic vitamin K if the patient is being treated for tuberculosis.
  Breastfeeding
    General Information
    Rifampin is compatible with breast-feeding. Guidelines encourage breast-feeding in individuals who are noninfectious and taking first-line tuberculosis agents, such as rifampin, as the small concentrations of these drugs measured in human milk have not been reported to produce adverse effects in the breastfed child. FDA-approved labeling recommends avoiding rifampin use during breast-feeding due to the potential to cause serious side effects such as tumorigenicity (based on animal studies) in the breast-fed child.

Interactions
Abacavir; Dolutegravir; lamiVUDine: (Major) Avoid concomitant use of dolutegravir and rifampin in integrase strand transfer inhibitor (INSTI)-experienced patients with INSTI-associated resistance substitutions or clinically suspected INSTI resistance. When used in other patients, increase the dose of dolutegravir to twice daily when administered with rifampin. For patients receiving a dolutegravir-containing, fixed-dose combination product, give an additional dolutegravir 50 mg dose separated by 12 hours from the combination product. Concomitant use may decrease dolutegravir exposure. Dolutegravir is a CYP3A substrate and rifampin is a strong CYP3A inducer. Coadministration with rifampin decreased dolutegravir exposure by approximately 50%.
Abacavir; lamiVUDine, 3TC; Zidovudine, ZDV: (Minor) Rifampin can accelerate the metabolism of zidovudine, causing a decrease in AUC of approximately 50%. However the effectiveness of zidovudine against HIV does not appear to be altered and no dosage adjustments are required.
Abemaciclib: (Major) Avoid coadministration of rifampin with abemaciclib due to decreased exposure to abemaciclib and its active metabolites, which may lead to reduced efficacy. Consider alternative treatments. Abemaciclib is a CYP3A4 substrate and rifampin is a strong CYP3A4 inducer. Coadministration with rifampin decreased the relative potency adjusted unbound AUC of abemaciclib plus its active metabolites (M2, M18, and M20) by approximately 70% in healthy subjects.
Abiraterone: (Major) Avoid the concomitant use of abiraterone and rifampin. If rifampin must be coadministered with abiraterone, increase the abiraterone dosing frequency to twice daily (i.e., 1,000 mg once daily to 1,000 mg twice daily). Reduce the dose back to the previous dose and frequency when rifampin is discontinued. Abiraterone is a substrate of CYP3A4; rifampin is a strong inducer of CYP3A4. Concomitant use may result in decreased concentrations of abiraterone resulting in reduced efficacy. In a drug interaction study, administration of abiraterone with rifampin decreased exposure of abiraterone by 55%.
Abrocitinib: (Major) Avoid coadministration of abrocitinib with rifampin as the combined exposure of abrocitinib and its 2 active metabolites may be decreased, resulting in reduced therapeutic effect. Abrocitinib is a CYP2C9 substrate and rifampin is a strong CYP2C9 inducer.
Acalabrutinib: (Major) Avoid the concomitant use of acalabrutinib and rifampin. If coadministration cannot be avoided, increase the acalabrutinib dose to 200 mg PO twice daily. Decreased acalabrutinib exposure occurred in a drug interaction study. Acalabrutinib is a CYP3A4 substrate; rifampin is a strong CYP3A4 inducer. In healthy subjects, the Cmax and AUC values of acalabrutinib were decreased by 68% and 77%, respectively, when acalabrutinib was coadministered with rifampin 600 mg/day for 9 days.
Acetaminophen: (Moderate) Concomitant use of acetaminophen with rifampin may increase the known risk of hepatotoxicity in relation to each drug. Severe hepatic dysfunction including fatalities were reported in patients taking rifampin with other hepatotoxic agents.
Acetaminophen; Aspirin, ASA; Caffeine: (Moderate) Concomitant use of acetaminophen with rifampin may increase the known risk of hepatotoxicity in relation to each drug. Severe hepatic dysfunction including fatalities were reported in patients taking rifampin with other hepatotoxic agents. (Minor) Rifampin is a potent inducer of the cytochrome P450 hepatic enzyme system and can reduce the plasma concentrations and possibly the efficacy of caffeine, including caffeine found in green tea products.
Acetaminophen; Aspirin, ASA; Caffeine: (Moderate) Concomitant use of acetaminophen with rifampin may increase the known risk of hepatotoxicity in relation to each drug. Severe hepatic dysfunction including fatalities were reported in patients taking rifampin with other hepatotoxic agents. (Minor) Rifampin is a potent inducer of the cytochrome P450 hepatic enzyme system and can reduce the plasma concentrations and possibly the efficacy of caffeine, including caffeine found in green tea products.
Acetaminophen; Aspirin: (Moderate) Concomitant use of acetaminophen with rifampin may increase the known risk of hepatotoxicity in relation to each drug. Severe hepatic dysfunction including fatalities were reported in patients taking rifampin with other hepatotoxic agents.
Acetaminophen; Aspirin; diphenhydrAMINE: (Moderate) Concomitant use of acetaminophen with rifampin may increase the known risk of hepatotoxicity in relation to each drug. Severe hepatic dysfunction including fatalities were reported in patients taking rifampin with other hepatotoxic agents.
Acetaminophen; Caffeine: (Moderate) Concomitant use of acetaminophen with rifampin may increase the known risk of hepatotoxicity in relation to each drug. Severe hepatic dysfunction including fatalities were reported in patients taking rifampin with other hepatotoxic agents. (Minor) Rifampin is a potent inducer of the cytochrome P450 hepatic enzyme system and can reduce the plasma concentrations and possibly the efficacy of caffeine, including caffeine found in green tea products.
Acetaminophen; Caffeine; Dihydrocodeine: (Moderate) Concomitant use of acetaminophen with rifampin may increase the known risk of hepatotoxicity in relation to each drug. Severe hepatic dysfunction including fatalities were reported in patients taking rifampin with other hepatotoxic agents. (Moderate) Monitor for reduced efficacy of dihydrocodeine and signs of opioid withdrawal if coadministration with rifamycins is necessary; consider increasing the dose of dihydrocodeine as needed. If the rifamycin is discontinued, consider a dose reduction of dihydrocodeine and frequently monitor for signs of respiratory depression and sedation. Rifamycins are inducers of CYP3A4, an isoenzyme partially responsible for the metabolism of dihydrocodeine. Concomitant use of dihydrocodeine with rifamycins can decrease dihydrocodeine levels, resulting in less metabolism by CYP2D6 and decreased dihydromorphine concentrations; this may result in decreased efficacy or onset of a withdrawal syndrome in patients who have developed physical dependence. (Minor) Rifampin is a potent inducer of the cytochrome P450 hepatic enzyme system and can reduce the plasma concentrations and possibly the efficacy of caffeine, including caffeine found in green tea products.
Acetaminophen; Caffeine; Pyrilamine: (Moderate) Concomitant use of acetaminophen with rifampin may increase the known risk of hepatotoxicity in relation to each drug. Severe hepatic dysfunction including fatalities were reported in patients taking rifampin with other hepatotoxic agents. (Minor) Rifampin is a potent inducer of the cytochrome P450 hepatic enzyme system and can reduce the plasma concentrations and possibly the efficacy of caffeine, including caffeine found in green tea products.
Acetaminophen; Chlorpheniramine: (Moderate) Concomitant use of acetaminophen with rifampin may increase the known risk of hepatotoxicity in relation to each drug. Severe hepatic dysfunction including fatalities were reported in patients taking rifampin with other hepatotoxic agents.
Acetaminophen; Chlorpheniramine; Dextromethorphan: (Moderate) Concomitant use of acetaminophen with rifampin may increase the known risk of hepatotoxicity in relation to each drug. Severe hepatic dysfunction including fatalities were reported in patients taking rifampin with other hepatotoxic agents.
Acetaminophen; Chlorpheniramine; Dextromethorphan; Phenylephrine: (Moderate) Concomitant use of acetaminophen with rifampin may increase the known risk of hepatotoxicity in relation to each drug. Severe hepatic dysfunction including fatalities were reported in patients taking rifampin with other hepatotoxic agents.

Severe hepatic dysfunction including fatalities were reported in patients taking rifampin with other hepatotoxic agents.
Acetaminophen; Chlorpheniramine; Phenylephrine : (Moderate) Concomitant use of acetaminophen with rifampin may increase the known risk of hepatotoxicity in relation to each drug. Severe hepatic dysfunction including fatalities were reported in patients taking rifampin with other hepatotoxic agents.
Acetaminophen; Codeine: (Moderate) Concomitant use of acetaminophen with rifampin may increase the known risk of hepatotoxicity in relation to each drug. Severe hepatic dysfunction including fatalities were reported in patients taking rifampin with other hepatotoxic agents. (Moderate) Concomitant use of codeine with rifampin can decrease codeine levels, resulting in less metabolism by CYP2D6 and decreased morphine concentrations; this may result in decreased efficacy or onset of a withdrawal syndrome in patients who have developed physical dependence. It is recommended to avoid this combination when codeine is being used for cough. If coadministration is necessary, monitor for reduced efficacy of codeine and signs of opioid withdrawal; consider increasing the dose of codeine as needed. If rifampin is discontinued, consider a dose reduction of codeine and frequently monitor for signs or respiratory depression and sedation. Codeine is primarily metabolized by CYP2D6 to morphine, and by CYP3A4 to norcodeine; norcodeine does not have analgesic properties. Rifampin is a strong CYP3A4 inducer.
